#7459. Solutions Architect at LiveKit

LiveKit is building the infrastructure layer for the agentic era of computing. Our platform gives developers everything they need to build, test, deploy, scale, and observe AI agents in production. Founded in 2021, LiveKit powers voice and agentic AI applications for OpenAI, Salesforce, Spotify, Meta, and tens of thousands of other developers, collectively facilitating billions of calls each year.

We're hiring a Solutions Architect (Pre-Sales) to be the technical partner to the engineers and enterprises building on LiveKit.

You'll work alongside an account executive to understand what a customer is trying to build, design the right architecture, and bring it to life with live demos and proofs-of-concept — guiding teams from first conversation to production.

This role is ideal for someone who loves to build, is energized by talking with other engineers, and takes pride in turning a hard technical problem into something that just works. Real-time voice and video is visceral — latency, quality, and reliability are felt, not read about — so the best conversations include something live. You'll collaborate closely with our Sales, Engineering, Product teams to win technical decisions and to make sure what you hear in the field informs how we build.

love to build and solve the most challenging customer problems with LiveKit

have a curious mind with a continuous desire to learn and improve

enjoy demoing and presenting, whether to a room or engineer, to a CTO or at a conference

have a team first mentality, seeking to lift others around you as you progress in your career

Partner with an account executive as the technical lead on strategic deals — from discovery and architecture design through demos, POCs, and technical decisions

Advise customers on cloud architecture and deployment across self-hosted and LiveKit Cloud (AWS, GCP, Azure)

Scope and run proofs-of-concept against clear, shared success criteria, guiding customers all the way to production

Create technical content — blog posts, guides, webinars, and conference talks — that helps developers and enterprises build with LiveKit

Comfortable with the modern deployment toolchain: containers, Kubernetes, infrastructure-as-code, and networking fundamentals (load balancing, DNS, firewalls/NAT)

Able to reason about connectivity, infrastructure, and latency end to end — how architecture, network path, compute, co-location, and model choice shape the AI agentic experiences of today

Strong programming ability in at least one of our core SDK languages — Python, TypeScript/Node, or Go

#7458. Meta Paused Its New Business Terms, And Businesses Should Plan As If It Never Gets Better via @sejournal, @slobodanmanic

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Spend less time on publishing and reports, and more on helpful content. November 18, 2 PM ET.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Meta removed ‘train’ from its crawl clause, kept ‘develop’, then paused everything. The pushback worked once. Do not expect it to work twice.

Meta backed down this time. On Nov. 5, it paused new business terms that would have let it crawl the website of every business using Facebook or Instagram, after publishers, a court, and a regulator pushed back. Pushback can work. But a business that gets its customers through Meta should plan as if Meta will never get better for it.

In October 2026, Meta previewed new business terms, the rules a business accepts when it uses Facebook or Instagram for business: a Page, ads, a shop. The first version let Meta use automated software to read the websites tied to a business, to “provide, develop, improve, research, train, and maintain” its products. Accepting was the price of staying, and the only way to say no was to stop using Meta for business.

On Oct. 16, Meta took the word “train” out and limited the crawl to its advertising and business products. “Develop” stayed in, and so did the crawl. On Oct. 23, Meta moved the start date from Oct. 30 to Nov. 20.

Meta doesn’t say why it paused. Paused is not withdrawn. Nieman Lab reports Meta’s notice says “our existing terms remain in effect”, and nothing in it says what comes back, or when.

Meta has done things that work for Meta since forever, with no regard for anyone. This is what Meta does. Hollywood has made two films about Facebook, and neither is about how well it treats its users.

Meta’s pattern feels like a slow death for a business in the Meta ecosystem, with nothing it can do about what Meta changes. Reaching for a business’s website is new. The attitude isn’t new: Meta not caring about its business users, and trying to give them no options.

So start investing in other acquisition channels, or at least planning for them.

Meta backed down this time. Do not wait for it to do it again.

This post was originally published on No Hacks.

Featured Image: Jack_the_sparow/Shutterstock

No. On Nov. 5, 2026, Meta paused them. Its Business Help Center says the updates to its Commercial Terms, Product Catalog Terms, Self-Serve Ad Terms, and Ad Creative Generative AI Terms will not take effect on Nov. 20, 2026, and Nieman Lab reports the notice says the existing terms remain in effect. Meta has not said what comes back, or when.

Under the terms Meta previewed in October 2026, a business using Facebook or Instagram for business would have agreed to let Meta’s automated software read its website for Meta’s ads and business products, with no opt-out short of leaving. Those terms are paused. A Meta spokesperson told Nieman Lab that robots.txt preferences are respected.

Yes. On Oct. 30, 2026 the U.K.’s Competition and Markets Authority opened a formal investigation into whether Meta’s commercial terms for businesses using its platforms breach competition law, under the Competition Act 1998. The CMA set no deadline and has made no decision.

#7457. Priceline’s AI agent Penny lands in Snapchat ads amid holiday rush

The brand is the first in the travel category to use AI Sponsored Snaps, which allow advertisers to bring their own chatbots into Snapchat.

Priceline is trying out Snapchat’s AI Sponsored Snaps in the ramp up to a holiday season when travel is expected to be more expensive due to higher fuel costs and stubborn inflation. Average domestic round-trip airfare for Thanksgiving was up 20% year over year on Priceline as of late October, Marketing Dive’s sister publication Hotel Dive recently reported. As consumers try to stretch their dollars further, more are turning to AI tools, including personal agents, to map out their trips and find deals.

Priceline has been shoring up a brand positioning around combatting soaring travel costs. In May, it brought back its Priceline Negotiator character in ads that showed William Shatner, who played the role in memorable TV commercials from the 2000s, passing the baton to Randall Park.

The Snapchat campaign is a way for Priceline to build awareness and adoption for Penny, a conversational AI tool that the brand revamped in June. Penny at the time added capabilities from Anthropic’s Claude models as part of a transition to becoming more “fully agentic,” including the ability to handle more complex user queries and surface real-time pricing comparisons.

Snapchatters who open Priceline’s promoted Snap message and engage with Penny will be able to seek out inspiration for travel destinations, develop an itinerary and identify places to stay. Those details will be preserved for consumers who then jump to Priceline to complete their travel booking. Penny converts users at approximately twice the rate of non-Penny users on Priceline, according to the company.

Snapchat introduced AI Sponsored Snaps in April, allowing advertisers to bring their own chatbots into a high engagement chat inbox interface on the ephemeral messaging app. The format is an iteration of Sponsored Snaps, a popular ad product that drives about 22% more conversions and 20% lower cost per action compared to other inventory on the platform.

Snapchat sees a bigger opportunity in the travel category, as two-thirds of surveyed users have added a destination to their wishlist after viewing it on Snapchat. Eighty-seven percent of Snapchat users make at least one trip for leisure annually, higher than non-users of the service, per press materials.

Snapchat for the past few years has been reworking its ad suite to be more centered on performance marketing solutions that can produce business results. The app has recently seen an exodus of advertising executives while its rate of ad revenue growth has fallen short of expectations, including due to declines in upper-funnel ad products. Snapchat’s revenue derived from ads increased 4% in Q3 while total revenue was up 14% for the period.

“While advertising revenue growth remains below our ambitions, advertisers are seeing the benefits of our performance improvements,” Snap CEO Evan Spiegel said in prepared remarks ahead of the company’s Q3 earnings earlier this month. “Our focus now is to turn these improvements into broader adoption, more meaningful share of advertiser budgets and more durable growth.”

#7455. Meta publishes explainer on Your Algorithm controls

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The feature enables users to opt in or out of certain topics and customize their feeds based on personal preferences.

Meta has shared some new insights into how its Your Algorithm control option on Facebook works, which enables users to provide direct input into what content they want to see more or less of in the app.

The tool enables users to add or remove specific topics from an automatically generated list of interests, which Facebook's system has built based on each user's in-app actions. In addition to removing and adding topics, users can also now tell the algorithm specifically what they want to see more of, via a recently added chatbot prompt.

Conceptually, this gives users more ways to refine their Facebook experience.

On the Meta engineering blog, the company has shared notes on how the process works, and explained more about how exactly updating user algorithm preferences can impact in-app experiences.

In addition to these topic-related notes, Meta said the system also used demotion rules to enact more general parameters that don’t relate to a specific topic.

“Quality-related requests, such as reducing AI-generated or sexually suggestive content, map to dedicated demotion signals rather than topic matching,” Meta said.

These demotion rules work differently than topic ranking, with the system using these as indicators to reduce these elements.

“During ranking, the same embedding-based comparison used in the relevance check removes candidates too close to an interest in Not Interested In, and demotion rules push down near matches,” Meta said.

In other words, this feature can reduce the presence of artificial intelligence-generated content in a user’s Facebook feed, though it won’t get rid of that entirely, and ranking will still be influenced by future engagement.

When a user requests less content from a specific creator, however, “that creator’s content is filtered out directly,” Meta said.

These notes provide more insight into how Meta plans to facilitate algorithm customization and give users more control over their in-app experiences.

Meta’s hope is that through tools like this, users will feel more empowered in the app. In turn, that may quell ongoing calls for the platform to return to a reverse chronological feed and remove ranking elements entirely.

Given that algorithmic amplification has continued to drive up Facebook and Instagram usage, Meta is very keen to keep that in place.

Though the company may not have a choice. Last month, the Australian government began preliminary investigations into potential new laws that would force social media platforms to provide users with an option to switch off algorithmic recommendations and set non-algorithmic feeds as the default.

A growing body of research suggests that algorithmic amplification can have harmful impacts. If this new push in Australia goes ahead and it ends up showing that the negative impacts of algorithms are reduced, that could lead to more regions exploring similar rules.

As noted, Meta is very keen to avoid this outcome.

Last year on a Q2 earnings call with analysts, Meta CEO Mark Zukerberga said AI recommendations had led to a 5% increase in time spent on Facebook, and a 6% boost on Instagram for the quarter.

On its Q2 earnings call a year later, Zuckerberg said that global time spent on Instagram had grown in the quarter by double digits year over year, and Facebook video time spent had increased by 9% globally, "largely driven by improvements to our feed and Reels recommendations."

It makes sense, then, that Meta is keen to avoid any external push to switch off its algorithmic ranking.

As such, Meta's hoping that the introduction of this control option will ease user concerns. Combine this with the fact that, based on past history, users rarely update their settings, and the company's likely hoping that it can benefit from the perception of control afforded by the tool, while also seeing a minor impact on its business.

#7454. Google Ads Channel Priorities Have Limits – What PMax Still Won’t Let You Split via @sejournal, @brookeosmundson

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Spend less time on publishing and reports, and more on helpful content. November 18, 2 PM ET.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

Curb unwanted Google Ads spending without losing valuable conversions by weighing bidding targets, Performance Max channel grouping, and allocation limits.

Google Ads’ new Channel Prioritization and Budget Allocation controls give PPC managers more input into decisions they’ve been leaving to automation. Advertisers can choose which eligible channels receive budget first and limit spending on lower-priority inventory.

The announcement gives advertisers more options, but choosing a setting still requires a reason to intervene. A campaign meeting its CPA or ROAS target may be spending in ways the business wants to change. Restricting that spending could address the concern, although it could also reduce conversions the advertiser would want to keep.

Before applying the new controls, PPC managers need to know what each setting can change and how they’ll judge the result. A shift in channel spending alone won’t establish that the account is performing better, especially if total conversion volume falls with it.

This article examines where advertisers gain control and how to decide whether an account needs an adjustment. Performance Max is worth digging into first because the channel breakdown advertisers can see in reporting goes further than the individual priorities these settings let them assign.

Google groups Search, YouTube, Discover, Gmail, and Maps together as “Google channels” under the new prioritization settings. Advertisers can prioritize that group separately from Search Partners and the Google Display Network, but they can’t assign different priorities within it.

If you’re a PPC manager reviewing Performance Max channel results, this limits the changes you can make directly. Seeing stronger returns from Search than YouTube doesn’t create an option to give Search priority access to the campaign budget.

The controls can address a concern about partner spending. Limiting Search Partners, however, still leaves Google deciding how to distribute spending across its owned channels. Advertisers shouldn’t expect the money withheld from partners to move specifically into Search.

If your business needs separate Search and YouTube budgets, that requirement still needs to inform campaign structure. Whether restructuring is worth the additional management depends on how firmly those budgets need to be separated.

Even where advertisers can assign separate priorities, the campaign-level bidding targets still apply. A campaign using Target CPA or Target ROAS won’t be allowed to buy more expensive inventory simply because the advertiser marked it high priority.

See also: Why Do Budgets Overspend Even With A Target ROAS or CPA? – Ask A PPC

A priority setting can affect allocation when channels compete for a limited campaign budget. With Target CPA or Target ROAS bidding, Google also considers whether additional spending can meet the target.

Say you’ve got a Search campaign that regularly exhausts its budget, and Google Search produces more qualified leads than Search Partners. Giving Google Search high priority lets you favor it when allocating that limited budget.

Google still evaluates the available traffic against the bidding target. If additional Search spending would exceed it, lower-priority inventory can receive budget when Google expects that inventory to meet the target.

That leaves you, the PPC manager, with a decision about how strongly you prefer Search. Your brand may be comfortable paying more for its leads, but changing the priority doesn’t communicate a willingness to accept a higher CPA. The bidding target would need a separate review at the campaign level.

Maximize Conversions and Maximize Conversion Value campaigns without a target work differently. Google funds high-priority inventory first, then makes remaining budget available to lower-priority channels when it cannot fully spend on the preferred inventory.

For Target CPA or Target ROAS campaigns without budget constraints, Google says priority tiers generally won’t change allocation. Allocation limits still apply.

Consider a campaign consistently spending $600 against a $1,000 average daily budget, without exhausting its budget on individual days. Lowering Search Partners’ priority may change little because both networks already have budget available.

If you’re wanting to limit partner spending in that campaign, you would first need to consider an allocation percentage. Before choosing one, check the dollar amount it represents: The percentage uses the configured budget, not actual spending.

Google calculates the allocation percentage using the campaign’s average daily budget. If the campaign spends less than that budget, a lower-priority channel can receive a larger share of actual spending than the percentage suggests.

In this example below, it keeps the budget and allocation settings unchanged:

In the second row, the channel receives 25% of actual spending without exceeding the $100 represented by the setting. The advertiser has set a spending allowance that won’t necessarily maintain a 90/ten split.

Google also applies allocation limits above 0% on a best-effort basis, so daily spending can exceed the intended amount. Google says a 0% allocation prevents spending on the channel.

Before saving a percentage, calculate what it allows in dollars and check that amount against the spending you intended. Revisit it when changing the campaign budget, too. Increasing the budget from $1,000 to $2,000 doubles the allowance under an unchanged 10% setting.

An increase intended to capture more Search demand could expand partner spending as well. When raising the campaign budget, check whether the existing allocation percentage still reflects what you’re willing to spend on that network.

The controls may be available in your account before you have a clear direction on how to use them. Start with the campaigns where network spending has raised a concern, and review the results before deciding what to change.

The checks below can help you choose where to apply a restriction and assess whether it improves performance.

See also: Google Ads Bid Strategy Testing: What Changed In 2026

Start by looking at what happens after someone converts. A network taking more budget than you expected may still be bringing in qualified leads or profitable sales.

In Search campaigns, use Network (with search partners) to compare results across networks. For Performance Max, go to Campaigns → Insights and reports → Channel performance and select the campaign.

If the campaign tracks several conversion actions, review them separately. Give leads enough time to qualify before judging their quality. The same CPA can look very different once the sales team follows up, as this made-up example shows:

Both networks bring in form submissions at $100 each, but qualified leads from Search Partners cost four times as much. Limiting that spending could help while the team investigates why fewer leads qualify. Sharing qualified lead or sales data with Google could also help bidding account for the difference.

For ecommerce, check how returns and product costs affect the sales each network brings in. A higher acquisition cost may be worth paying if those customers are more profitable in the long run.

Start with a campaign where you’ve identified a reason to change network-level spending. Applying the same settings across the account could affect campaigns that are already performing well.

Before making the change, note the campaign’s budget and bidding target so you can track what changed. Decide what you need to see afterward. Using the lead example above, that might mean spending less on unqualified inquiries without losing qualified leads.

In Search, open Campaign settings → Channel Prioritization to set campaign-specific priorities and an optional allocation percentage. Check which controls are available for the campaign type you’re working on.

Avoid changing bidding targets or targeting at the same time, where possible. Otherwise, it will be harder to tell what the channel setting accomplished.

If the campaign uses a shared budget, check the other campaigns using it before saving. The allocation applies across that shared budget, so the change can affect more than the campaign you opened.

Account-level defaults can save time once you know the same approach makes sense for other campaigns. Until then, a campaign-level change gives you a smaller starting point to review.

Search campaigns with Display enabled need a settings review before Google retires the existing Display Expansion allocation setup in February 2027.

To keep the current configuration, Google recommends setting Display to Low priority with a 50% daily budget allocation. That percentage reflects the existing setup; it isn’t Google’s recommended allowance for a new Display test.

Find the Search campaigns with Display enabled and check how much they currently spend on that network. If you want to maintain the setup, use Google’s recommended migration settings and monitor spending after the change.

You can choose a lower allocation if the results justify it. Note that you’re intentionally reducing Display’s allowance, so anyone reviewing performance knows the campaign settings changed beyond the migration itself.

Once the settings have had time to affect ad delivery, the channel spending report can help show what changed. A larger Search share might look encouraging, but it could simply mean partner spending fell while Search spending stayed flat.

That could still be a good result. In the lead generation example, spending less on unqualified leads would help if qualified lead volume held steady. For ecommerce, a drop in spend would be less appealing if profitable sales disappeared along with it.

Those results may take longer to show up than the spending change itself. Leads still need follow-up, and recent clicks may turn into purchases later. The first few days of reporting may say more about the new spending mix than its effect on the business.

Google says the controls can be used in custom experiments, but experiments with multi-campaign shared budgets aren’t supported, and in those cases all channels are treated as the same priority tier. If your campaign runs on a shared budget, a before/after comparison may be your only option.

A before/after comparison can still help, although a promotion or seasonal change could explain some of the difference. The channel settings won’t necessarily deserve all the credit, or blame.

The limit may be worth keeping if it cuts wasted spend without hurting sales. If it saves money but costs the business profitable customers, loosening it could be the better choice.

See also: When Attribution Looks More Precise Than The Data Behind It

PPC managers already decide how much they’re willing to spend on different networks. Until now, acting on those decisions has sometimes meant excluding inventory or changing campaign structure. These settings give advertisers another option when they want to limit spending without shutting a network off entirely.

That could make it easier to revisit an earlier exclusion or address a spending concern within an existing campaign. Google still decides how to allocate much of the budget, particularly across its owned channels in Performance Max. The additional control gives advertisers more room to manage that spending, even if it doesn’t reach every decision they’d like to make.

#7453. Customer Growth Manager at Uscreen

Uscreen is the video monetization platform for businesses building recurring revenue from video content. Our customers replace a patchwork stack of separate hosting, community, courses, email, and app vendors with one branded experience across web, mobile, and TV.

The platform is only half of it. We believe growth requires behavior, not just software. Customers don't stall because a feature is missing. They stall because they priced wrong, never built an email list, or launched an offer nobody wanted. Coaching is how we fix that, and it's part of the product rather than an add-on.

You own a segment, and you run it like a growth marketer.

We have a cohort of smaller video businesses where retention is not where it should be. Nobody owns it today. You will. The revenue that stays in that segment, and the revenue that grows inside it, is yours.

This is a coaching role for someone with a growth marketer's mindset. We wouldn't hire a demand gen marketer who couldn't analyze the state of the business and figure out what's needed, and we won't hire a coach who can't either. You'll look at a customer's business the way an owner would: where the revenue comes from, where it leaks, and what to do next. You pull your own numbers to get there. When a customer needs a custom report to answer a growth question, you build it yourself.

It's a one-to-many job. Your cohort is larger than a 1:1 book, so the work is designing coaching that scales: cohort programs, group sessions, office hours, and triggered outreach when the data says a business is drifting. You'll build that motion rather than inherit it.

Success is measured in dollars: gross revenue retention in your cohort first, expansion second. Not tickets closed, not calls booked.

A coach with a growth marketer's mindset. Maybe you've run growth or demand gen for a small business, built your own audience, or coached founders through their numbers. You understand how a membership business makes money, you pull your own analytics to see what's working, and you're comfortable doing it in front of a client. What matters is that you can find the real constraint in a business, then sit with the operator and say the honest thing.

Benefits may vary depending on location and local regulations. Final eligibility and coverage details will be shared during the hiring process.

#7452. Netflix embraces 35mm film in international campaign

“Find the Thing You Never Knew You Needed” consists of three new spots shot in different aspect ratios and a social series.

As U.S. consumers continue to cut back on subscriptions, including for streaming services, Netflix’s latest campaign emphasizes the quality of its vast content library in order to help it stand out in a crowded field. “Find the Thing You Never Knew You Needed” comes as company executives continue to put an emphasis on value and engagement following price hikes and lower projected ad revenue.

Created by Netflix’s in-house shop the Brand Creative Studio and agency Mother, the campaign features three spots shot on 35mm film using different aspect ratios. In “Axolotl,” which is shot for widescreen, an axolotl leverages its quadrupedalism to escape its tank and turn the TV back on after its owners leave in the middle of a “Bridgerton” marathon. “Dawn” follows a vampire who braves the rapidly rising sun to find a working TV in order to watch “Love Is Blind.” In “Hypersleep,” an astronaut who is the only one awake while his fellow shipmates are in cryosleep, wakes someone up in order to show them the “Soda Pop” scene from “KPop Demon Hunters.”

The spots were directed by Vania Heymann and Gal Muggia of the production company Iconoclast. The nature of the ads allows for the featured program to be swapped out depending on the market. In Brazil, the show featured in “Dawn” is crime drama “Os Donos Do Jogo,” or “Rulers of Fortune” in English.

Over the course of four episodes, the social series “Typecast” will feature four creatives and showcase their somewhat unexpected Netflix fixations. The series kicks off with fashion designer Willy Chavarria and his show of choice “Love on the Spectrum.” Other guests include musician Teezo Touchdown, makeup artist Isamaya Ffrench and Randy Savvy of The Compton Cowboys. The videos were filmed in 16mm to fit the feel of the overall campaign.

The campaign’s use of analog film is part of a recent trend that has seen advertisers looking to run counter to the rise of artificial intelligence. Stop-motion animation has been of particular interest, with brands such as Levi’s and Prada embracing the artform. Netflix’s spots, which feature custom-built sets, follow a similar vein of appealing to the desire for human-made advertising.

Netflix grew revenue 12% year over year to $12.9 billion in Q3, per an earnings release. The company lowered its expected ad revenue for the year from $3 billion to $2.7 billion, a drop it attributed to a slower-than-expected rollout of some advertising capabilities.

#7451. Google Discovers Unexpected Patterns In AI Image Generation via @sejournal, @martinibuster

#7450. X experiments with expanded AI transparency labels

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The company is testing an automated way to detect and tag posts that may have been generated using artificial intelligence.

X is developing new AI transparency labels for posts, which will not only indicate whether AI was used to generate an update, but also how it was applied in each instance.

Michael O’Herlihy, X’s head of brand safety, shared some examples of these new labels during a presentation at the Brand & AI Safety Summit in New York on Tuesday.

As shown on these example screens, posted by Adweek senior tech reporter Kendra Barnett on X, X’s new AI content labels will include more detailed information on why a post was flagged for AI content. This will better explain the rationale X’s automated enforcement system is using, while also giving creators more opportunities to refute these labels by offering them a more detailed understanding of any potential concerns.

The hope is that these expanded notes will help X refine its AI detection process by prompting more creators to provide feedback on the specifics. This information can then be used to train the system.

At the same time, the tags will add more transparency for users, who may be less likely to re-share a post that’s been flagged for AI content.

O’Herlihy added that X increased its reliance on automated tagging of AI violations because it’s much faster. The automation should also ensure that more harmful content is refuted in-stream.

It could be a good way to enhance transparency in the AI era. However, false positives could also be problematic for creators, who could get annoyed if their content is unfairly tagged.

#7449. Visibility Gets You On The List, Evidence Helps You Stay There via @sejournal, @billhunt

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Spend less time on publishing and reports, and more on helpful content. November 18, 2 PM ET.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

AI visibility can hide evidence gaps that weaken buyer recommendations as requirements get specific, making decision readiness a crucial GEO metric.

For much of the past two years, the AI search conversation has focused on visibility. Teams want to know whether their brand is mentioned, where it appears in recommendations, how often competitors surface, and whether they are being cited across ChatGPT, Gemini, Claude, Perplexity, Google, and other AI surfaces.

Those are useful metrics, but I have increasingly questioned whether they tell us enough about the corresponding business outcome.

Nearly two years ago, I began using the term “eligibility gates” to describe the detailed decision criteria that determine whether a company or product remains qualified as a buyer moves through the decision process. I later expanded on that idea when I wrote Decision Coverage: whether an organization has exposed enough information and evidence for AI to evaluate, compare, qualify, and confidently recommend.

That led to two related arguments tied to previous articles. In my article Beyond Content Parity, I argued that using tools and processes that simply match competitor content may create parity without creating any decision advantage. In Beyond Brand Sovereignty, I argued that the knowledge AI needs often exists beyond the marketing site, scattered across product, sales, support, engineering, legal, customer success, and external sources.

A recent project allowed me to test those ideas against an actual business problem.

The company did not have an AI visibility problem. It frequently appeared in AI-generated consideration sets. The concern was what happened afterward, when prospective buyers continued the conversation and began asking more specific questions about requirements, workflows, integrations, outcomes, evidence, and competitive differences.

The question was not “How do we get seen in AI?” It was, “Why, with all this visibility, is our lead pipeline shrinking?” The reality we found was that as AI took a prospective buyer further down the consideration set, they became less competitive.

To prove my thesis and identify the problem, we created 38 detailed, criteria-based prompts across several product categories and buyer segments, and evaluated them on five AI surfaces, yielding 190 responses.

These were intentionally different from broad “best vendor” prompts. They reflected the kinds of requirements buyers use to narrow a field: workflow fit, integrations, regulatory requirements, interoperability, operational conditions, analytics, platform breadth, and other decision criteria. I had to create a detailed explanation of their purpose as diagnostic-only because the brand and their SEO agency argued that no human would ever use that complex a prompt. We actually found that some prompts were even longer after talking to actual procurement managers.

The initial numbers looked excellent – even better than their current visibility scorecard. Our research found the company was mentioned in 98.4% of evaluated responses and appeared as either a primary recommendation or shortlisted option 93.2% of the time. For many GEO programs, that would be the headline of the week and champagne for everyone. The brand was almost always visible and almost always treated as a serious option. But visibility was not the problem we were investigating.

When we evaluated those same responses against the decision criteria in the prompts, 73.7% had weak or missing evidence supporting important claims or recommendations about the company. In 15.8% of runs, an important association, decision criterion, competitive distinction, or discovery signal contained what we classified as a Significant or Critical Gap. The more we refined our evidence-scoring criteria, the higher this number rose.

The critical finding was that AI systems generally knew the company belonged in the conversation. However, they were far less consistent at substantiating why it should remain there once the buyer began applying specific eligibility criteria. That is the distinction that matters and explains their declining lead pipeline.

There is a meaningful difference between asking which vendors operate in a market and asking which vendors can satisfy a specific combination of technical, operational, regulatory, and business requirements.

A model may know a company is a legitimate vendor, understand its main products, and have enough general information to put it on a shortlist. But once the buyer asks how an integration works, whether a workflow is supported, what happens under a particular operating condition, what limitations apply, or what evidence supports an outcome claim, the information requirements become more demanding. Those are not merely additional keywords. They are evidence requirements.

That explains how a brand can achieve a 98.4% mention rate and 93.2% primary-or-shortlist rate while still having weak or missing evidence in nearly three-quarters of the responses. The numbers are not contradictory. They measure different stages of the decision process.

No one can dispute that AI visibility gets the company into the consideration set. Decision readiness determines whether AI has enough information to keep it there.

See also: B2B Buyers Choose A Vendor Before They Reach Out – 3 Ways To Be Visible When It Counts

One of the more important findings was that weak evidence did not necessarily mean the company lacked the capability. In many cases, the capability appeared to exist, but the available information did not explain or substantiate it well enough to satisfy the AI surface’s confidence level.

A marketing bullet might claim an end-to-end process without ever showing the actual process, connections, or how the data flows between systems. We found efficiency claims but did not have any “proof” from internal tests or customer testimonials of the efficiency gain.

Sometimes the information was simply fragmented. A model might need to connect a product page to an integration page, then to another interoperability page, before it could infer how the pieces satisfied the buyer’s requirement. While an AI system could or should be able to make those multi-part connections, each additional inference creates another opportunity for incomplete representation or for a competitor with clearer evidence to become the safer recommendation.

We also found that the strongest content was not necessarily the longest or most buzzword-integrated, but rather content that made the relationships among capability, workflow, buyer requirements, and outcome explicit.

An interesting finding was that only first-party evidence was not inherently a weakness. Many claims lacked third-party validation, but this was not necessarily flagged as a defect. We found that authoritative product documentation may be the best evidence that a feature or integration exists. However, every claim involving superiority, effectiveness, adoption, scale, measurable outcomes, or competitive differentiation did benefit from independent corroboration by customers, industry, government, analysts, or other parties.

That means some evidence gaps are not content-writing problems at all, but does anyone else agree with the evidence?

See also: Schema, LLMs & The Low Bar For ‘Evidence’ In GEO

The findings reinforced the concern I raised in my earlier work about how easy-button AI content-generation systems create “me-too content parity.” As I explained to this brand, most of the GEO efforts to date were what I would call SEO-adjacent GEO. It is a newly rebranded SEO process that leverages AI speed for traditional content-gap analysis. Teams look at competitor coverage, People Also Ask questions, common prompts, and pages receiving citations, then create content to close those gaps. There is nothing inherently wrong with that. In this case, it appears to have worked extremely well at establishing this company’s category presence. The limitation appears when everyone says roughly the same thing, and you don’t offer details.

If every vendor claims to be end-to-end, highly integrated, AI-enabled, scalable, flexible, and focused on efficiency, those statements may help establish that each belongs in the category. They do much less when the model has to determine what “end-to-end” covers, what “integration” connects, what the AI actually does, or what evidence supports the efficiency claim.

Parity can help a company qualify for the conversation without providing enough information to survive deeper evaluation.

See also: AI Gives You The Vocabulary. It Doesn’t Give You The Expertise

Running the prompts was relatively straightforward. There wasn’t a tool or off-the-shelf framework for what we were trying to measure. We needed to build a multi-variable evidence scorecard capable of determining whether the model had enough support to evaluate the company against the criteria that actually mattered in the buying decision.

That meant defining what counted as strong evidence, what first-party documentation was enough, and when independent corroboration became important. The bigger challenge was thinking beyond the “we are great” organizational mindset to determine when an evidence gap or omission was serious enough to affect consideration or selection. That turned out to be even harder than getting SEOs to agree on ranking factors.

Those judgments then had to be translated into a repeatable scoring system and applied consistently across the prompts and five AI surfaces. It also required conversations beyond content. Sales had to help identify criteria and objections that affect deals. Product had to validate capabilities and limitations. Customer-facing teams helped identify real outcomes. In some cases, customers themselves were the best source for understanding what evidence mattered.

This is why I do not believe there will be one universal GEO evidence scorecard that every organization can download. The methodology can be reusable if you can honestly define the decision criteria and what constitutes sufficient evidence, then evaluate whether AI can connect the brand to those criteria.

As we evaluated eligibility criteria and evidence requirements, many findings no longer looked like conventional content gaps. Looking at this massive evidence gap matrix as one manager exclaimed, “that no human will ever read,” it was obvious this would become a team effort to make the changes. Content teams cannot make up content, nor will they necessarily know how everything works.

Some of the knowledge required to close these gaps is organizational in nature. We had to pry it out of product, engineering, and sales, and then run it through marketing and compliance. In some cases, we had to loop in external and partner relations when stronger third-party or attributable proof was required beyond what we could create.

The content team still has a critical role: organize that knowledge, make relationships explicit, create coherent evidence paths, and expose information in ways humans and AI systems can understand.

Most importantly, this project validated and reinforced my belief that measuring only the first AI response is insufficient. True, those broad PAA-like prompts are valuable for generating a list of options worth considering. The problem is the current tools cannot maintain state like a human workflow and aggregate the sequence of prompt turns most users leverage. Each subsequent prompt digs deeper into the criteria for consideration, maintaining preferences, and memory, and culminating in the multivariate result that I affectionately call prompt math.

This means that GEO measurement needs to become more sophisticated and account for both visibility and decision readiness. Many tools now have answer accuracy monitoring, which is a step forward. I argue that we need to put more effort into the element that is much closer to the business problem organizations should actually care about.

There is an obvious danger here because once marketers conclude that AI systems need evidence, we will probably see an explosion of “evidence-shaped content”: longer pages, superficial diagrams, weak surveys, synthetic research, and AI-generated case studies created primarily because someone believes those formats will influence an LLM, just like the explosion of Reddit submission services. That misses the point. The objective is not to manufacture artifacts that appear persuasive to AI, but to provide real content that reduces uncertainty and risk for the buyer.

If the information would not help a knowledgeable customer make a better decision, it probably should not exist merely to improve a GEO score.

As noted, the company had already achieved what many GEO programs would consider an amazing success: 98.4% visibility and 93.2% primary-or-shortlist consideration, yet 73.7% of those same responses had weak or missing evidence. Unfortunately, our analysis could not prove that this evidence gap caused every lost sale or every subsequent AI exclusion. What it does show is that a visibility-only measurement model would have missed the weakness we were asked to investigate.

Any company can succeed in entering the conversation but fail to offer enough credible evidence to remain qualified as the conversation becomes more specific.

#7446. Carnival gamifies cruise experience via interactive Twitch livestream

Streamer iJustine will take on a series of challenges voted on by viewers as she explores a digital Carnival Celebration ship.

Carnival is turning to Twitch to give consumers a unique look at what a trip aboard one of its vessels would entail at a time when the demographics of the average cruise passenger are in flux. The activation comes as the company reported record-high bookings for 2027 and a net income of $1.9 billion in the third quarter, an all-time high for the company. However, gross margin yields fell 1.3%  due to high fueling costs, per an earnings release.

Cruising has become more popular than ever, with a record 22.4 million Americans expected to take a cruise in 2027, up 3.6% year over year, per AAA research. The pre-pandemic total for American cruise travelers was 14.2 million. While the vacation choice is stereotypically associated with older demographics, the average age of an American cruiser is 45.8, and millennials and Gen Z travelers are increasingly drawn to the travel method due to lower costs and value-oriented packages, according to AAA research.

The livestream will feature a custom-built game in which iJustine, who has nearly 55,000 followers on Twitch at press time, will tackle challenges on the simulated Carnival Celebration. The ship’s spaces and activities, as well as destinations from the brand’s Paradise Destinations collection, will be part of the gameplay. Along with input from the audience, Weigand will help keep energy high by introducing challenges, passing on requests from viewers and offering community instructions. iJustine, who will be playing live onboard, will work to earn her cruise director badge and a Fun Meter will track energy levels.The game is over once the meter hits zero.

To encourage participation, audience members will have the chance to win a cruise vacation. The full livestream will be posted to Instagram and TikTok the day after the event so fans can catch up on what they missed. The game will not be made available to the public after the stream.

“We’re constantly looking for new ways to push the boundaries on how we connect with consumers, and Play Carnival takes that thinking to a whole new level,” said Michael Sotelo, vice president of brand, product and social media at Carnival Cruise Line, in press materials.

The effort falls under the “Find Your Fun Again” campaign, which encourages consumers to ditch the stress and monotony of everyday life and find joy aboard a Carnival ship. Actor Nick Offerman took center stage in a spot in which he goes on vacation after smashing his phone due to receiving an overload of notifications.

Amazon-owned platform Twitch has become a go-to for brands looking to experiment with connecting with young consumers. Last year, E.l.f. Cosmetics became the first brand to test Twitch’s livestream shopping ads.

#7445. Elon Musk’s Grok AI faces legal challenges over nudification

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Multiple plaintiffs allege that the chatbot is directly responsible for creating naked images based on photos taken of them as children, per Bloomberg.

Elon Musk’s xAI is facing questions regarding who is ultimately responsible for the generation of harmful content via generative artificial intelligence tools. At the heart of these issues is whether the fault lies with the person who tells the system to create this material, or the platform that facilitates this functionality.

These problems lie at the core of multiple legal cases involving xAI’s Grok chatbot, with plaintiffs in three U.S. states seeking legal recourse against xAI over the creation of CSAM material.

As reported by Bloomberg, xAI has come under investigation in three high-profile cases.

The company has denied responsibility in these cases, and said these depictions were generated by users, not by xAI itself.

Musk, xAI’s owner, has also made similar public statements.

In January, amid reports that Grok was being used to generate thousands of nudified images every day, Musk posted: “Anyone using Grok to make illegal content will suffer the same consequences as if they upload illegal content.”

However, shortly after making that announcement, and facing a potential ban of X apps from app stores, xAI changed its policies and usage restrictions on Grok to ensure that it could no longer be used to generate nudified images at scale.

The change in approach showed that xAI had the capacity to restrict this functionality whenever it wanted. However, the company instead initially refused to restrict Grok usage on free speech grounds, and settled on user autonomy as its legal defense.

Indeed, xAI has even challenged new laws designed to outlaw the generation of non-consensual naked images.

As reported by Reuters last month, xAI succeeded in blocking the implementation of Minnesota’s proposed ban on AI-generated naked images by arguing that the law was unconstitutional and infringed on free speech rights. The company was the only provider actively opposing this law.

At the same time, xAI has repeatedly touted Grok’s systematic capacity to generate what is called “spicy” content or offensive material in its “unhinged mode.” The company has also launched companion chatbots that can engage in NSFW chats.

As such, this does seem like more of a feature than a bug, yet xAI is hoping it can hang onto the fact that it’s not liable for usage that goes beyond certain stated parameters.

Establishing legal responsibility could set a new precedent in AI image generation, and it will be interesting to see whether any of these cases results in xAI being held responsible.

#7444. AI Shopping Errors: What To Fix To Help Shoppers Choose Your Products

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Spend less time on publishing and reports, and more on helpful content. November 18, 2 PM ET.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Explore the future of retail with AI shopping. Understand how AI affects product visibility and decision-making for consumers.

This post was sponsored by Writesonic. The opinions expressed in this article are the sponsor’s own.

When shoppers ask ChatGPT or Google what to buy, answers often come back as product cards with prices, ratings, and “buy now” links. AI writes those cards from data spread across the web, but few brands know how those cards position their products.

That’s a risky blind spot to have heading into the holidays. Adobe forecasts AI traffic to US retail sites will grow 130% this holiday season.

AI shopping results usually go wrong in three ways. Your product doesn’t show up, it shows up with the wrong details, or a competitor or reseller gets the final click instead of you. The good news is that these errors trace back to product data you control.

This guide covers what these results look like, why they go wrong, and how to audit your own products.

AI shopping results are product recommendations that appear inside an AI answer. They’re usually triggered by a specific buying question, like “waterproof hiking boots for wide feet under $150.”

In March 2026, OpenAI expanded its Agentic Commerce Protocol (ACP) to cover product discovery. Merchants now share product feeds through ACP so their catalogs appear in ChatGPT.

Retailers like Target, Best Buy, and Wayfair are already integrated. Shopify merchants flow in through Shopify Catalog.

OpenAI dropped in-chat Instant Checkout in March 2026 and now lets merchants run checkout on their own sites. ChatGPT helps you get the shopper, but your product page still has to close the sale.

AI Overviews now show product cards for many shopping searches, drawing on Google’s Shopping Graph. Merchants add their products mainly by uploading feeds to Google Merchant Center. Google says the Shopping Graph holds over 50 billion product listings.

AI Mode and Gemini use the same Shopping Graph, and Google is rolling out agentic checkout there too. In this scenario, Google’s agent buys a tracked item with Google Pay once the shopper confirms. To qualify, merchants need a Merchant Center account and a Universal Commerce Protocol (UCP) integration.

The most common AI shopping issues fall into three groups.

Most brands find these issues by accident. Someone runs a prompt, sees a wrong price, and fixes that one product. Hundreds of other prompts go unchecked.

The common thread is consistency. When your feed, product page, and retailer listings disagree on price, stock, or naming, AI has to pick one. It doesn’t always pick right.

An analysis reported by Search Engine Journal tracked ChatGPT product picks that came from integrated feeds. In one day in July 2026, their share went from 8.26% to 61.54%. Of 517 tracked stores with visibility swings of 33% or more, 450 saw drops.

Merchant variety shrank too. The top 10 merchants’ share of references grew from 22.5% to 41.8%. Stores without a connected feed now risk losing picks they used to win.

Your product also lives on Amazon, Walmart, Target, and other retailers. Each listing can show a different price, title, or stock status. AI may link to whichever one looks most complete.

Product schema, plus identifiers like Global Trade Item Number (GTIN), manufacturer part number (MPN), brand, and variant, tells AI exactly which item it’s looking at. Without them, AI can mix up models, sizes, and colors.

AI leans hard on what third-party websites say about your brand and products. Writesonic’s June 2026 study of 2,000+ brands found YouTube or Reddit mentions made a brand about three times more likely to show up in AI answers. Mentions on its own site raised that just 1.3 times.

Manual audits break down fast. Each check is only a snapshot, and AI answers change constantly.

Results vary by platform, region, and day, so rerun the same prompts over time. Daily checks give you the most accurate picture. Audit at the category level too, because a brand can dominate headphones and be invisible in speakers. How do you do this? A strong AI Visibility platform will help you collect, understand and action this data.

Writesonic’s study of 631,999 prompt runs across seven AI platforms found the top-ranked brand in ChatGPT rotated on 52% of prompts when the same prompt was rerun.

Writesonic’s AI Shopping Tracker automates those checks daily across ChatGPT and Google AI Overviews. It also shows how often your prompts trigger shopping results at all, which manual audits miss.

It maps directly to the problems covered above:

Any tracker only sees the prompts you give it, so the prompt set from your audit is the foundation. Start with your best sellers and the categories you can’t afford to lose this season.

The brands that check what AI says about their products, and fix the data behind it, will own these product cards. Everyone else is letting a model decide how their products are shown.

#7443. Partner Solutions Architect , AI Builder at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

Supabase is the backend of choice for a growing wave of AI-native builders, and we're deepening structured partnerships with the platforms they build on top of — Anthropic, OpenAI, Vercel, and Lovable. These partners' teams are talking to thousands of developers and agents who need exactly what we build—a full backend platform with Postgres at the core—without the vendor lock-in. This is an opportunity to get Supabase in front of the right partner SAs and make us the default recommendation for a modern, AI-ready backend.

We’re looking for a Platform Solutions Architect to join our Success Team and help embed Supabase into the Solutions and Engineering communities with AI Builders.

You’ll work closely with Partner Solutions Architects and Partner Engineers at Anthropic, OpenAI, Vercel, and Lovable, helping us proactively identify builder problems where Supabase is the right solution and closing the gap between partner intent and real developer outcomes. This role is ideal for someone who thrives in async, fast-paced environments and is excited about building developer tools that scale to millions of AI-native apps and agents.

In this role, you’ll focus on three core pillars:

Build deep relationships with Technical counterparts at Anthropic, OpenAI, Vercel, and Lovable, focusing initially on defined use cases and agent/app-migration motions.

Develop and deliver technical workshops, training materials, and enablement content to give partner SAs the fluency to position Supabase.

Create demos, integration guides, and reference architectures that make Supabase’s fit with Anthropic, OpenAI, Vercel, and Lovable immediately legible.

Partner with SAs at these companies on active opportunities to move deals forward and feeding technical pipeline intelligence back to the alliance lead.

Resolve blockers for partner SAs working on Supabase implementations to maintain their credibility with the builders they support.

Communicate clearly across both technical and non-technical audiences, explaining complex concepts with ease.

#7441. Why A Dashboard Cannot Define Your Brand’s AI Visibility Strategy via @sejournal, @TaylorDanRW

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Spend less time on publishing and reports, and more on helpful content. November 18, 2 PM ET.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

Keep your AI search strategy independent of unstable tools by owning your prompts and raw data, so vendor changes don't erase your measurement history.

One of the best things about the capabilities of AI is how easily it turns complex, multi-source data into dashboards anyone can understand.

Couple this with the opportunity of creating new tools and platforms for the AI search space, and you end up with this culmination of AI search tools and the need to fill a marketing channel void. The risk is that we reach for dashboards for comfort, especially when we are facing unknowns.

Marketing leadership loves the comfort of a software dashboard.

Search has always operated on a predictable, standardized framework when it comes to tools and platforms. You license an enterprise suite, import your target queries, track deterministic positions from one to one hundred and build quarterly reports that stakeholders can understand at a glance.

When generative search emerged, we naturally sought that same software-first playbook. Marketers looked for a platform to define what AI brand visibility should look like.

When you let software lead your AI strategy, you anchor your organization to an immature, volatile software market. And which tool do you choose with so many new offerings?

I researched Product Hunt, G2, and LinkedIn, and found 194 tools that have launched over the past two years that focus on aiding brands to improve their AI visibility.

Looking at the AI platform landscape, the number of tools and the speed at which they are being launched creates a false illusion of category maturity. Breaking down the 194 tools from this sample across the last two to three years highlights that a little over 60% of them are lightweight bootstrap experiments, side projects, and solo founder builds. The mortality rate of a startup mindset is running between 55% and 60%. This does not include the ones I have not been able to identify and track that do not have a presence on these platforms. Every month, many tools currently switch off APIs, stop renewing domain names, or pivot into unrelated generative wrapper apps.

The main driver of this is the impact of cost. In traditional search monitoring, scraping for our own tracking purposes has been relatively stable and has only become more volatile in recent years. But pinging a search engine and parsing HTML markup costs a fraction of what it takes to fire complex multi-turn prompt matrices when you have live web browsing enabled.

Then, factor in how Google is actively working to combat this practice, meaning that you have to continue reinvesting and adjusting how your stack works to provide the same service. Inference calls, headless browsers, anti-bot bypass proxies, and context tokens all add up relentlessly, especially as your business becomes more successful and scales.

For a self-serve tool charging $100 a month, running comprehensive weekly prompt variations for a large enough sample across a client’s core product catalog, margins are non-existent. The moment an underlying model provider updates its inference pricing, tightens its rate limits, or changes its interface, these projects really show how fragile they are and break down economically and operationally.

See also: AI Visibility Isn’t A Sham – Most Ways Of Measuring It Are

Another trap with allowing a tool to lead your AI strategy is approaching it the same way you would a vendor. Tools often come with multi-year or multi-term contracts. Once that contract is up, or if a better offer comes along from another provider, you will want to move away. It is often assumed that you can just export the data and migrate to the next platform.

We know from traditional SEO tooling that everything is measured slightly differently. While they have become relatively homogenized over the years, this is still a very immature marketplace. There is no standard or uniform underlying measurement of what AI visibility actually means. So when we swap one AI visibility tool for another, it does not mean that we are swapping like for like. It means we are swapping divergent philosophies of measurement and meaning.

Because there is no universally accepted standard for measuring AI and conversational output, every tool invents its own proprietary synthetic index that it also markets as being its main differentiator and USP.

See also: The AI Search Metrics I Use To Track Client Pipelines

From my experience and industry sentiment across social media, there is substantial executive pressure to track AI, meaning these tools are filling an inherent need created within organizations. They will generate an initial audit, take a screenshot of a brand mention for a board deck, and then face the daily reality of stochastic noise. Sources cited inside generative responses can suffer huge amounts of citation drift with high levels of variance.

This occurs without any changes made to the brand’s website or competitors. It is simply the model experiencing variation. Approaching this the same way as a rank tracker means you would spend hours investigating phantom drops and random gains caused by inference temperature shifts or model hallucinations.

Letting a tool dictate the metrics inevitably forces teams and management to track tool idiosyncrasies rather than business fundamentals.

See also: Your AI Visibility Tracker Is Quietly Breaking Your Analytics And Your Strategy

Software cannot be the anchor of your AI search strategy. Strategic stability comes from decoupling your execution from specific vendors. This is easier said than done, and third-party tools do offer an easier path without needing to engineer your own solutions. However, there are core elements you need to take ownership of.

You need to own the query matrix internally and not let a third-party platform decide which queries matter. You need to form your own internal stacks of prompts based on customer intent and categories, high-value comparative queries, and category-defining questions. You need to store this query set in internal documentation belonging to your organization, independent of any external subscription.

You also need to own experimental prompt stacks, brand prompt stacks, and other core elements so that you can track variation across a set and start to measure your own citation drift rates. This is not the same approach as traditional rank tracking.

Try to find external platforms that offer raw webhook exports, BigQuery connectors, or standardized CSVs. You want to extract all response text, timestamp data, source URLs, and prompt inputs directly into your own data infrastructure. If your vendor decides to change its calculation logic, your historical raw observations will remain intact.

Importantly, you need to recognize visibility as an outcome and not an input. A tracking dashboard does not generate any authority.

LLMs do not reference brands because the company bought visibility software. In my experience, they reference brands that possess dense authoritative entity definitions, unambiguous architectures and schemas and active digital footprints.

The software landscape for conversational and generative search will take years to reach maturity. Consolidations will accelerate, venture capital subsidies will dry up, and the vast majority of tools will vanish. Do not construct your executive roadmap around the features of an unstable tool category.

#7440. LinkedIn tests frequent viewer feature

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Premium users already see who views their profile, but a new report from Wired said the platform may also soon show how often someone stops by.

LinkedIn users may soon be able to derive more value from its “Who viewed your profile” list, with the platform working on a new feature that would not only show who visited their profile, but also how often each specific user revisited their page within a given period.

That would add more context to LinkedIn’s profile viewer data, although for most users, this is fairly useless information. However, some marketers have highlighted this as a lead-generation opportunity, providing a list of potential warm contacts, based on the fact that they’ve checked out a user’s profile.

A frequency indicator on the same display would enhance that capacity, by showing people who have returned to a user’s profile in the app again and again, which could be an indicator of even stronger interest.

So long as that interest is in a professional capacity.

A visit count for each profile listed could also show users who could be interested in them for other reasons, which could make this a somewhat unsettling display.

LinkedIn users can lock down their profile to connections only, so there would be ways to address this concern, and of course users are under no obligation to contact people who’ve visited their profile.

Wired said that LinkedIn confirmed the feature is currently in testing, but it had no further info to share at this stage.
